I’ll make excuses about availability or say I have something at home, but really it’s because I don’t want to be in the building after dark. I also push back on scheduling those overnight or late-night releases when I can, even when I know professionally that they make sense.\n\nAnd if I do have to go in, I don’t use the main lobby if it’s dark out. I take the side entrance, or I try to arrange to work from home instead. The lobby is the worst part for me. Walking through it after dark just… it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. So yes, I’ve definitely been avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of anxiety or unease when you're reminded of the break-in, such as seeing something that looks like the lobby where it happened?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not even always something obvious, which is what makes it feel so frustrating and kind of embarrassing.\n\nLike, if a badge scanner beeps a certain way, especially when it’s quiet, my whole body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. Or if I hear heavy footsteps coming down a hallway behind me, I’ll suddenly feel like I’m back there in the office that night. It’s like the room I’m actually in drops away for a second.\n\nThe late-night deployment alerts do it too—the notifications, the wording, even just seeing one pop up can make my chest tighten. And then this image cuts in, really sharply, of the guard on the floor. I can be in the middle of writing something or talking to someone, and it just… interrupts everything. I don’t want to think about it, but it’s like my brain throws me right back there anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at work as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not all the time, but there are moments where it feels less like I’m remembering it and more like I’m suddenly back in it.\n\nIt can happen really fast. Like if a badge scanner beeps in that sharp way, or if I hear heavy footsteps in the hallway behind me, especially when it’s quiet, my body reacts before I can even tell myself where I am. My chest gets tight, and for a second it’s like I’m not just at work anymore—I’m back in that office that night.\n\nThe late-night deployment alerts can do it too. I’ll see one come through, and it’s like everything around me kind of drops away. And then the image of the guard on the floor just cuts right through whatever I’m doing. I can be in the middle of a completely normal task, and suddenly that’s what I’m seeing, and I have to remind myself that it’s not happening again. But in the moment, it really can feel like it is.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted by these feelings and reactions?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say it was just uncomfortable and I’m still performing the same, but that wouldn’t really be true.\n\nMy work has slipped in ways that are starting to show. I’ve missed migration deadlines, which is not like me. I used to be the person who had the timeline mapped out, who caught the dependency issues early, who made sure the handoff was clean. Lately I either push things off, or I avoid the parts of the project that might involve being there late, especially deployments, and then everything stacks up. I’ve skipped or traded out of key late deployments that I really should have been part of, and I know that affects the team.\n\nIt’s gotten noticed, too. In my last performance review, I was called out for conflicts and reliability issues that honestly never used to happen with me. That was hard to hear, because I’ve always taken a lot of pride in being steady at work. I feel embarrassed even saying that, because I know how it sounds, like I’m making excuses, but it really has changed how I show up.\n\nAnd outside of my core job duties, I’ve pulled back a lot. I used to mentor junior analysts and be involved in women-in-tech leadership events, and now I avoid those too. Not because I stopped caring—I still care a lot—but because I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to be in that role for other people. I worry I’ll cancel, or seem distracted, or just not be able to handle being there the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely affecting work and daily life. More than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try not to, and honestly I’ve built a lot of my week around not having to face it.\n\nI’ll tell myself it’s practical scheduling or workload management, but a lot of the time it’s really that I don’t want to be in that building after dark, and I definitely don’t want to walk through that lobby when it’s quiet. I’ve started trading shifts with people, or asking if someone else can cover a release window, or I’ll push back on timing if I can justify it at all. Sometimes I work from home when technically I probably could go in, because the idea of going through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart.\n\nAnd if I do have to go in, I’ll take the side entrance even if it’s less convenient. I know that probably sounds small, like just using a different door, but it feels like this whole mental calculation every time—how do I avoid seeing the place, how do I avoid setting myself off, how do I get through the day without thinking about it. So yes, I avoid thinking about it, but also I avoid the situations that make me think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're somehow defective or inadequate because of what happened during the break-in?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. More than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s like… I know, rationally, that what happened was something that happened to me, not something I caused. But I still walk around with this feeling that something in me got broken or exposed afterward. Like the version of me who was competent and calm and good at handling pressure—who got promoted because people trusted me—is just not really there in the same way anymore.\n\nAnd that’s embarrassing to say, because my whole sense of myself at work was built around being capable. I was the person who could stay late, troubleshoot, make decisions, keep things moving. Now I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Why can’t you just do your job? Why are you reacting like this? Other people would have moved on by now.” So yes, I do end up feeling defective. Not in a dramatic way exactly, but in this constant, quiet way, like I’m damaged now.\n\nAnd I think part of why I’m here is because I can’t shake this belief that work isn’t safe anymore, but also that I’m not the same person anymore. Like I’m inadequate somehow because I can’t just snap back into being who I was before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know some of it probably isn’t fair to myself, but I still feel guilty.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, like if I had insisted beforehand that we needed better security for the overnight deployment, maybe things would have gone differently. I remember thinking even before that night that being there late felt kind of exposed, and I didn’t push hard enough. I didn’t make it a big issue. And now I think, why didn’t I say something louder, or put it in writing, or refuse to do it that way?\n\nAnd then there’s the part about calling sooner. That one really gets to me. I was hiding and I was terrified, and part of me knows that—I really do know that. I wasn’t thinking clearly. I was just trying to stay quiet and not be found. But then I think about the guard being hurt, and I keep wondering if I had called sooner, even by a few minutes, maybe he wouldn’t have been hurt so badly.\n\nSo yes, I feel guilty. Not exactly because I think I caused it, but because I can’t stop finding these places where I feel like I failed to prevent it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that you're a failure or that you're worthless because of what happened during the break-in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “worthless” feels like a harsh word, and I don’t know that I’d say it exactly that way all the time, but I do have thoughts that I’m a failure. Or that something in me has failed.\n\nI keep comparing myself to who I was before the break-in. Before, I was the competent one. I was the person people trusted, the person who got promoted because I could handle pressure and make decisions and keep things under control. And now it feels like that person isn’t really there anymore, or she’s there but I can’t access her the same way. Like I look normal from the outside, I can still answer emails and sit in meetings, but inside I keep thinking, “You’re not who they thought you were. You’re damaged now.”\n\nAnd I hate saying that because I know it sounds like I’m being dramatic or unfair to myself, but it’s there. I’m here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and at the same time I can’t shake the feeling that I’m somehow broken because I can’t just be the same capable person I was. So yes, I do get those thoughts—not always as “I’m worthless,” but more like, “I’m inadequate now. I failed somehow. I’m not the person I was supposed to be.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or relationships outside of work because of how you've been feeling since the break-in?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people in some obvious, deliberate way, like deciding, “I’m cutting everyone off.” It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nI guess I have noticed that I’m less… available, maybe? Like if someone asks to do something, I sometimes just don’t have the energy to explain where my head is, so I might say I’m tired or busy. But I don’t know if that counts as avoiding social interactions, or if it’s just me trying to get through things. I still talk to people. I’m not completely isolated or anything.\n\nRelationships outside work haven’t been the main thing I’ve been focused on, honestly. Most of what I notice is tied to work and how I feel there. So… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m quieter or less eager to make plans, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly been avoiding relationships. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty messed up since then.\n\nMost nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but as soon as it’s quiet my brain starts replaying these little details from work. Not even always the biggest parts, sometimes it’s tiny things, like the way the room felt or where I was positioned, and then I’m back in it mentally. I’ll lie there going over it, trying to tell myself I’m at home and it’s over, but my body doesn’t really believe that.\n\nAnd I’ve been waking up from dreams too. A lot of them are some version of me hiding under the conference table again, trying not to make noise. I’ll jolt awake like I’ve just been caught or like I need to get out, and then it takes a while to settle down enough to even know where I am.\n\nEven on nights when I technically sleep six or seven hours, it doesn’t feel restorative. I wake up feeling like I never really powered down, like part of me stayed alert the whole time. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reminded of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling of being outside my body, like I’m watching myself from across the room or anything. At least, not in a way I could clearly point to and say, “Yes, that’s what happened.”\n\nThe “dream” part is a little harder for me to answer, because when I’m reminded of it, things can feel unreal in the sense that I get kind of pulled into the memory or I feel disconnected from what’s happening right in front of me for a moment. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I literally believe I’m dreaming. It’s more like I’m not fully present, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed myself floating outside my body or observing myself from somewhere else. If anything, it’s more that I get overwhelmed or mentally stuck on it, and then it takes me a second to reorient. But I’m not sure whether that counts as what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I know that might sound dramatic, because it’s “just work,” and I’m a computer systems analyst, so normally I’m pretty logical about things. I used to feel competent there—like I had earned my place, especially after getting promoted—but now it’s like that version of me is gone or fake somehow.\n\nI keep thinking there’s something wrong with me now, like I’ve been damaged in a way I can’t quite explain. And I don’t know how to get back to feeling like the person who could handle things. That’s what’s been bothering me enough that I finally thought I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Was there something specific that happened at work that made you start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after the break-in at work, during an overnight deployment. That was about two years and four months ago now, which is strange to say because part of me feels like it was ages ago, and part of me feels like everything changed from that point.\n\nI was there because we had this deployment window overnight—one of those things where it has to happen when systems are quieter—and then the break-in happened. Since then, I haven’t really been able to feel the same about being at work. I keep marking time from that night, honestly. Like, before that I was one person, and after that… I don’t know. But yes, that’s the specific thing. It’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places or situations related to your work since the break-in?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… more than I probably wanted to admit at first.\n\nI’ve started trading shifts with people if there’s any chance I’d have to be there late, especially for release windows or deployments. I’ll make excuses about availability or say I have something at home, but really it’s because I don’t want to be in the building after dark. I also push back on scheduling those overnight or late-night releases when I can, even when I know professionally that they make sense.\n\nAnd if I do have to go in, I don’t use the main lobby if it’s dark out. I take the side entrance, or I try to arrange to work from home instead. The lobby is the worst part for me. Walking through it after dark just… it makes me feel like I’m going to come apart. Like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. So yes, I’ve definitely been avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of anxiety or unease when you're reminded of the break-in, such as seeing something that looks like the lobby where it happened?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not even always something obvious, which is what makes it feel so frustrating and kind of embarrassing.\n\nLike, if a badge scanner beeps a certain way, especially when it’s quiet, my whole body reacts before I’ve even had time to think. Or if I hear heavy footsteps coming down a hallway behind me, I’ll suddenly feel like I’m back there in the office that night. It’s like the room I’m actually in drops away for a second.\n\nThe late-night deployment alerts do it too—the notifications, the wording, even just seeing one pop up can make my chest tighten. And then this image cuts in, really sharply, of the guard on the floor. I can be in the middle of writing something or talking to someone, and it just… interrupts everything. I don’t want to think about it, but it’s like my brain throws me right back there anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at work as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Yes. Not all the time, but there are moments where it feels less like I’m remembering it and more like I’m suddenly back in it.\n\nIt can happen really fast. Like if a badge scanner beeps in that sharp way, or if I hear heavy footsteps in the hallway behind me, especially when it’s quiet, my body reacts before I can even tell myself where I am. My chest gets tight, and for a second it’s like I’m not just at work anymore—I’m back in that office that night.\n\nThe late-night deployment alerts can do it too. I’ll see one come through, and it’s like everything around me kind of drops away. And then the image of the guard on the floor just cuts right through whatever I’m doing. I can be in the middle of a completely normal task, and suddenly that’s what I’m seeing, and I have to remind myself that it’s not happening again. But in the moment, it really can feel like it is.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted by these feelings and reactions?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I wish I could say it was just uncomfortable and I’m still performing the same, but that wouldn’t really be true.\n\nMy work has slipped in ways that are starting to show. I’ve missed migration deadlines, which is not like me. I used to be the person who had the timeline mapped out, who caught the dependency issues early, who made sure the handoff was clean. Lately I either push things off, or I avoid the parts of the project that might involve being there late, especially deployments, and then everything stacks up. I’ve skipped or traded out of key late deployments that I really should have been part of, and I know that affects the team.\n\nIt’s gotten noticed, too. In my last performance review, I was called out for conflicts and reliability issues that honestly never used to happen with me. That was hard to hear, because I’ve always taken a lot of pride in being steady at work. I feel embarrassed even saying that, because I know how it sounds, like I’m making excuses, but it really has changed how I show up.\n\nAnd outside of my core job duties, I’ve pulled back a lot. I used to mentor junior analysts and be involved in women-in-tech leadership events, and now I avoid those too. Not because I stopped caring—I still care a lot—but because I don’t feel steady enough or reliable enough to be in that role for other people. I worry I’ll cancel, or seem distracted, or just not be able to handle being there the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely affecting work and daily life. More than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try not to, and honestly I’ve built a lot of my week around not having to face it.\n\nI’ll tell myself it’s practical scheduling or workload management, but a lot of the time it’s really that I don’t want to be in that building after dark, and I definitely don’t want to walk through that lobby when it’s quiet. I’ve started trading shifts with people, or asking if someone else can cover a release window, or I’ll push back on timing if I can justify it at all. Sometimes I work from home when technically I probably could go in, because the idea of going through that lobby at night makes me feel like I’m going to come apart.\n\nAnd if I do have to go in, I’ll take the side entrance even if it’s less convenient. I know that probably sounds small, like just using a different door, but it feels like this whole mental calculation every time—how do I avoid seeing the place, how do I avoid setting myself off, how do I get through the day without thinking about it. So yes, I avoid thinking about it, but also I avoid the situations that make me think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're somehow defective or inadequate because of what happened during the break-in?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. More than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s like… I know, rationally, that what happened was something that happened to me, not something I caused. But I still walk around with this feeling that something in me got broken or exposed afterward. Like the version of me who was competent and calm and good at handling pressure—who got promoted because people trusted me—is just not really there in the same way anymore.\n\nAnd that’s embarrassing to say, because my whole sense of myself at work was built around being capable. I was the person who could stay late, troubleshoot, make decisions, keep things moving. Now I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Why can’t you just do your job? Why are you reacting like this? Other people would have moved on by now.” So yes, I do end up feeling defective. Not in a dramatic way exactly, but in this constant, quiet way, like I’m damaged now.\n\nAnd I think part of why I’m here is because I can’t shake this belief that work isn’t safe anymore, but also that I’m not the same person anymore. Like I’m inadequate somehow because I can’t just snap back into being who I was before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know some of it probably isn’t fair to myself, but I still feel guilty.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, like if I had insisted beforehand that we needed better security for the overnight deployment, maybe things would have gone differently. I remember thinking even before that night that being there late felt kind of exposed, and I didn’t push hard enough. I didn’t make it a big issue. And now I think, why didn’t I say something louder, or put it in writing, or refuse to do it that way?\n\nAnd then there’s the part about calling sooner. That one really gets to me. I was hiding and I was terrified, and part of me knows that—I really do know that. I wasn’t thinking clearly. I was just trying to stay quiet and not be found. But then I think about the guard being hurt, and I keep wondering if I had called sooner, even by a few minutes, maybe he wouldn’t have been hurt so badly.\n\nSo yes, I feel guilty. Not exactly because I think I caused it, but because I can’t stop finding these places where I feel like I failed to prevent it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that you're a failure or that you're worthless because of what happened during the break-in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “worthless” feels like a harsh word, and I don’t know that I’d say it exactly that way all the time, but I do have thoughts that I’m a failure. Or that something in me has failed.\n\nI keep comparing myself to who I was before the break-in. Before, I was the competent one. I was the person people trusted, the person who got promoted because I could handle pressure and make decisions and keep things under control. And now it feels like that person isn’t really there anymore, or she’s there but I can’t access her the same way. Like I look normal from the outside, I can still answer emails and sit in meetings, but inside I keep thinking, “You’re not who they thought you were. You’re damaged now.”\n\nAnd I hate saying that because I know it sounds like I’m being dramatic or unfair to myself, but it’s there. I’m here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and at the same time I can’t shake the feeling that I’m somehow broken because I can’t just be the same capable person I was. So yes, I do get those thoughts—not always as “I’m worthless,” but more like, “I’m inadequate now. I failed somehow. I’m not the person I was supposed to be.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or relationships outside of work because of how you've been feeling since the break-in?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people in some obvious, deliberate way, like deciding, “I’m cutting everyone off.” It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nI guess I have noticed that I’m less… available, maybe? Like if someone asks to do something, I sometimes just don’t have the energy to explain where my head is, so I might say I’m tired or busy. But I don’t know if that counts as avoiding social interactions, or if it’s just me trying to get through things. I still talk to people. I’m not completely isolated or anything.\n\nRelationships outside work haven’t been the main thing I’ve been focused on, honestly. Most of what I notice is tied to work and how I feel there. So… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m quieter or less eager to make plans, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly been avoiding relationships. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty messed up since then.\n\nMost nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but as soon as it’s quiet my brain starts replaying these little details from work. Not even always the biggest parts, sometimes it’s tiny things, like the way the room felt or where I was positioned, and then I’m back in it mentally. I’ll lie there going over it, trying to tell myself I’m at home and it’s over, but my body doesn’t really believe that.\n\nAnd I’ve been waking up from dreams too. A lot of them are some version of me hiding under the conference table again, trying not to make noise. I’ll jolt awake like I’ve just been caught or like I need to get out, and then it takes a while to settle down enough to even know where I am.\n\nEven on nights when I technically sleep six or seven hours, it doesn’t feel restorative. I wake up feeling like I never really powered down, like part of me stayed alert the whole time. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reminded of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling of being outside my body, like I’m watching myself from across the room or anything. At least, not in a way I could clearly point to and say, “Yes, that’s what happened.”\n\nThe “dream” part is a little harder for me to answer, because when I’m reminded of it, things can feel unreal in the sense that I get kind of pulled into the memory or I feel disconnected from what’s happening right in front of me for a moment. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I literally believe I’m dreaming. It’s more like I’m not fully present, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed myself floating outside my body or observing myself from somewhere else. If anything, it’s more that I get overwhelmed or mentally stuck on it, and then it takes me a second to reorient. But I’m not sure whether that counts as what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you think about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from what I was trying to describe before. I don’t think I feel disconnected from myself in a dramatic way, like I don’t know who I am or I’m not in my own body. That hasn’t really been my experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nWith my surroundings… maybe sometimes I get kind of quiet or spaced out when something makes me think about the break-in, but I don’t know if I’d call it being disconnected. It’s more like my attention narrows, and I’m focused on the memory or the feeling of it instead of whatever is actually happening around me. Then after a moment I sort of come back and realize I wasn’t really paying attention.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed a clear sense of being detached from reality or from myself. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there is one part that’s really hard for me to account for.

I remember hiding under the conference table. I remember being very aware of trying not to move, trying not to breathe too loudly, and I can remember certain details from that part almost too clearly. And then I remember the police being there afterward. Like, I remember realizing there were officers in the building and that it was over, or supposed to be over.

But the section where they actually came into the conference room and got me out is… blank. Not completely everything around it, but that specific transition. It’s like I know it happened because obviously I didn’t stay under the table, and people have referred to it, and I have these before-and-after pieces. But when I try to picture the actual moment of them finding me or me getting up or walking out, it’s like the file is corrupted. I keep trying to open it and there’s just nothing there, or only vague flashes that I don’t really trust.

It bothers me because I feel like I should remember that part. It seems like the kind of thing my brain would hold onto, but instead it’s just missing. I can remember hiding, and I can remember police being there, but that middle part is just… not accessible, no matter how hard I try.

I remember thinking even before that night that being there late felt kind of exposed, and I didn’t push hard enough. I didn’t make it a big issue. I was hiding and I was terrified, and part of me knows that—I really do know that. I wasn’t thinking clearly. I was just trying to stay quiet and not be found. But then I think about the guard being hurt, and I keep wondering if I had called sooner, even by a few minutes, maybe he wouldn’t have been hurt so badly.\n\nSo yes, I feel guilty. Not exactly because I think I caused it, but because I can’t stop finding these places where I feel like I failed to prevent it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts that you're a failure or that you're worthless because of what happened during the break-in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, “worthless” feels like a harsh word, and I don’t know that I’d say it exactly that way all the time, but I do have thoughts that I’m a failure. Or that something in me has failed.\n\nI keep comparing myself to who I was before the break-in. Before, I was the competent one. I was the person people trusted, the person who got promoted because I could handle pressure and make decisions and keep things under control. And now it feels like that person isn’t really there anymore, or she’s there but I can’t access her the same way. Like I look normal from the outside, I can still answer emails and sit in meetings, but inside I keep thinking, “You’re not who they thought you were. You’re damaged now.”\n\nAnd I hate saying that because I know it sounds like I’m being dramatic or unfair to myself, but it’s there. I’m here because I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, and at the same time I can’t shake the feeling that I’m somehow broken because I can’t just be the same capable person I was. So yes, I do get those thoughts—not always as “I’m worthless,” but more like, “I’m inadequate now. I failed somehow. I’m not the person I was supposed to be.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or relationships outside of work because of how you've been feeling since the break-in?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people in some obvious, deliberate way, like deciding, “I’m cutting everyone off.” It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nI guess I have noticed that I’m less… available, maybe? Like if someone asks to do something, I sometimes just don’t have the energy to explain where my head is, so I might say I’m tired or busy. But I don’t know if that counts as avoiding social interactions, or if it’s just me trying to get through things. I still talk to people. I’m not completely isolated or anything.\n\nRelationships outside work haven’t been the main thing I’ve been focused on, honestly. Most of what I notice is tied to work and how I feel there. So… maybe a little, in the sense that I’m quieter or less eager to make plans, but I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly been avoiding relationships. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sleep has been… pretty messed up since then.\n\nMost nights I get into bed and I’m exhausted, but as soon as it’s quiet my brain starts replaying these little details from work. Not even always the biggest parts, sometimes it’s tiny things, like the way the room felt or where I was positioned, and then I’m back in it mentally. I’ll lie there going over it, trying to tell myself I’m at home and it’s over, but my body doesn’t really believe that.\n\nAnd I’ve been waking up from dreams too. A lot of them are some version of me hiding under the conference table again, trying not to make noise. I’ll jolt awake like I’ve just been caught or like I need to get out, and then it takes a while to settle down enough to even know where I am.\n\nEven on nights when I technically sleep six or seven hours, it doesn’t feel restorative. I wake up feeling like I never really powered down, like part of me stayed alert the whole time. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when you're reminded of the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had that exact feeling of being outside my body, like I’m watching myself from across the room or anything. At least, not in a way I could clearly point to and say, “Yes, that’s what happened.”\n\nThe “dream” part is a little harder for me to answer, because when I’m reminded of it, things can feel unreal in the sense that I get kind of pulled into the memory or I feel disconnected from what’s happening right in front of me for a moment. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I literally believe I’m dreaming. It’s more like I’m not fully present, if that makes sense.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed myself floating outside my body or observing myself from somewhere else. If anything, it’s more that I get overwhelmed or mentally stuck on it, and then it takes me a second to reorient. But I’m not sure whether that counts as what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you think about the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from what I was trying to describe before. I don’t think I feel disconnected from myself in a dramatic way, like I don’t know who I am or I’m not in my own body. That hasn’t really been my experience, at least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nWith my surroundings… maybe sometimes I get kind of quiet or spaced out when something makes me think about the break-in, but I don’t know if I’d call it being disconnected. It’s more like my attention narrows, and I’m focused on the memory or the feeling of it instead of whatever is actually happening around me. Then after a moment I sort of come back and realize I wasn’t really paying attention.\n\nBut I don’t want to overstate it. I haven’t noticed a clear sense of being detached from reality or from myself. It’s more like I get absorbed in it or distracted by it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering certain parts of what happened during the break-in at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… there is one part that’s really hard for me to account for.\n\nI remember hiding under the conference table. I remember being very aware of trying not to move, trying not to breathe too loudly, and I can remember certain details from that part almost too clearly. And then I remember the police being there afterward. Like, I remember realizing there were officers in the building and that it was over, or supposed to be over.\n\nBut the section where they actually came into the conference room and got me out is… blank. Not completely everything around it, but that specific transition. It’s like I know it happened because obviously I didn’t stay under the table, and people have referred to it, and I have these before-and-after pieces. But when I try to picture the actual moment of them finding me or me getting up or walking out, it’s like the file is corrupted. I keep trying to open it and there’s just nothing there, or only vague flashes that I don’t really trust.\n\nIt bothers me because I feel like I should remember that part. It seems like the kind of thing my brain would hold onto, but instead it’s just missing. I can remember hiding, and I can remember police being there, but that middle part is just… not accessible, no matter how hard I try."
  }
